Does a graph database belong in the agent harness? What the held-out test found
When an agent changes one file, can a tool put the other files and definitions it will need in front of it? Seven tools answered the same 418 change-completion tasks from 75 real commits of July and August 2026 that no setting was tuned on, each with an 8,000-token answer. Tags on every number say whether it is
held-out
(the one fair run) or
tuning
(where settings were chosen, so optimistic); engine times carry
timing run
(tuning inputs only), and other people's figures or your session records
outside source
.
Theme
auto
light
dark
Data written 2026-11-13; every number on this page is read from one checked data file.

What this page answers, and what it does not
It answers two questions.
Do three storage engines differ
: SQLite (relational), LadybugDB (graph) and the DuckDB graph layer, holding the same facts?
Does a parser-built code graph beat a scripted search
at finding the other code that a real change touched? It does
not
test a layer of meaning that a model reads from documents (what graphify adds), facts with time validity, cached conclusions with invalidation, orientation (summaries and maps), an agent actually using any of these tools, or the tokens a working session spends.
K: what you asked that this test did not cover →
✕
An agent using the tool; tokens in a session
no agent was run

✕
Orientation
summaries, reports, slice maps
NOT TESTED
✕
Time validity and cached conclusions
facts valid from and to a commit; conclusions invalidated when their facts change
NOT TESTED
✕
Meaning read from documents by a model
rules, reasons, document-to-code links: what graphify adds
NOT TESTED
✓
Structure: a parser-built code graph
files, functions, routes, tables, settings, tests, changed-together history; held in three engines

A
What did the test find?
How to read it.
Seven cards, one finding each, most important first. The big number is a difference between two tools. The line under it is its 95 % interval (the range the true difference probably lies in); the grey band is ±0.05, the smallest difference in recall the test treated as worth acting on; the dark vertical line is zero. A line that crosses zero means the test cannot tell the two apart. Hover any mark for its exact value.
1 · the engine
Same facts, same method: the same answers in all three engines
0.000
recall difference [0.000, 0.000]
held-out, 75 commits
same within ±0.05
LadybugDB minus SQLite
DuckDB minus SQLite
The three implementations compute the same answers from the same facts with the same method: the answer shown to the reader was identical in SQLite (relational), LadybugDB (graph) and the DuckDB graph layer on 770 of 770 tasks. The engine did not change the answer.
See E: does the engine matter
2 · a graph-only ranking
Adding a community prior gave no gain of 0.05 or more
+0.015
recall [−0.010, +0.042]
held-out, 75 commits
no gain of 0.05 or more
stored grouping
draw 1
draw 2
draw 3
draw 4
Adding a stored community prior to personalised PageRank (LadybugDB) gave no gain of 0.05 recall or more. The five community draws (diamonds) range from +0.009 to +0.032. On tests to update it was lower: −0.037 [−0.083, +0.007]. The PageRank iterations ran in application code; only the grouping came from the engine.
See E: the five draws
3 · typed store against scripted search
A typed store found more of the change, but only for changes that start in the mobile app
+0.076
recall [+0.036, +0.118]
held-out, 75 commits
better by the pre-declared rule
all 75 commits
mobile app
elsewhere
Same answer size (8,000 tokens) for every tool, in either kind of engine. Top line: all changes. Hollow blue: seeds in the mobile app, +0.151 [+0.097, +0.207] over 31 commits. Grey: seeds elsewhere, +0.024 [−0.023, +0.075] over 48 commits, which cannot be told apart from zero. It also beat both floors; against the changed-together floor (+0.063 [+0.019, +0.110]) the gain again sits in mobile seeds and elsewhere it ties (+0.001 [−0.051, +0.056]). Both lower ends are under 0.05. The search arm is a fixed script, not an agent searching.
See D: where the gain comes from
4 · what moved the score
How far the walk goes and which facts are stored mattered; the ranking algorithm barely did
0.346
tuning recall moved by hop depth
tuning, 65 commits, optimistic
hops: 1 → unbounded
every ranking mode (unbounded)
Upper bar (blue dots): hop depth, from 0.438 at 1 hop to 0.784 unbounded. Lower bar (violet): every ranking mode at unbounded depth, all within 0.023. Following edges one way only cost up to 0.215; adding the history of files that changed together added 0.056.
See F: try the hop control
5 · code-reading questions
Typed queries scored higher, but not provably on all 30 questions
+0.100
F1 [−0.060, +0.257]
held-out, 30 questions
inconclusive
30
23
22
Lines from top: all 30 questions; the 23 first listed as untouched; the 22 whose handling never changed, where the interval is [+0.025, +0.309] and excludes zero. Final answers were about the same size (median 31 against 29.5 tokens); to produce its answer the scripted search read a median of 694.5 tokens. The typed side's own costs (knowing the schema, writing a query program per kind of question) were not counted.
See G: every question
6 · engine speed
Every engine answers in milliseconds; one model step takes seconds
32.7 ms
SQLite (relational), selected method, warm median per ranking
timing run, 5 passes, tuning inputs only
milliseconds, not tokens
SQLite 32.7 ms
LadybugDB 71.2 ms
DuckDB 98.7 ms
On the selected method SQLite was fastest: 32.7 ms against 71.2 ms for LadybugDB (graph) and 98.7 ms for the DuckDB graph layer on the frozen graph (bars: spread between passes). On the PageRank variants the arm that runs PageRank in application code (LadybugDB) was fastest, which compares implementations, not graph operators. One model step takes about 6.6 s, so engine speed does not enter the token bill.
See H: engines
7 · not tested
Whether an agent finishes work faster with any of this
not tested
no agent was run
Every result here is about what a tool puts in front of the reader within a fixed answer size. Whether an agent then finishes a task faster, or with fewer mistakes, was not measured.
See J: what could be tested next
Your five questions, answered in one line each
Does the way a graph stores things change the answer?
No. Given the same facts and the same method, SQLite, LadybugDB and DuckDB returned the same answers (770 of 770 tasks). The facts in the store and the method decide the answer.
E
Are the answers scored for the value they carry?
Partly. A changed file or definition counts only when the tool actually showed it inside the 8,000-token answer, and a definition only when its own lines were shown. Every item counts the same; how much an agent gains from it was not measured.
B
Do the type of graph, the algorithms and the number of hops matter?
Hops and which facts are stored moved tuning recall by up to 0.346; ranking algorithms by at most 0.023; the one graph-only algorithm that reached the final gave no gain of 0.05 or more.
F
Was the graph given a fair, optimised form against an established relational engine?
Each engine had 60 tuned configurations on the same 65 tuning commits, its own native algorithms were tried, and audits fixed graph-side defects before scoring.
E
Would it be more token efficient?
Every tool filled the same 8,000-token answer, so the test compares how much each found per token, not tokens saved: the typed store found more, on mobile-app changes. Engine times are milliseconds and do not enter the token bill; an agent's tokens were not measured.
C
G
H
K
What it shows.
The engine did not change the answer, and on the selected method SQLite (relational) answered fastest, in milliseconds that do not enter the token bill. A typed store of connections found more of a change than a scripted search at the same answer size, and the whole gain sits in mobile-app changes. No agent was run; what you asked that this did not cover is in K.
B
What was compared, and how was each answer scored?
How to read it.
The cards are the seven tools: what each reads and how it answers. The three typed stores in the dashed box hold the same facts and run the same method. Below them, one task as a picture. At the bottom, step through three real tasks with Back and Next.
Typed stores: the same facts (files, functions, routes, tables, settings, tests and how they connect, including which files changed together before) in three engines
SQLite (relational)
typed store
Reads
Only the facts built from the parent commit of each task (never the task commit or anything later).
Answers
A recursive SQL query walks from the seed file and symbol over the typed edges, scores every reached file, and the shared packer prints the best files first as a path line plus the signature lines of their definitions until the token budget is used.
What it is
The extracted facts (typed nodes such as files, functions, routes, tables and settings, and typed edges such as imports, calls, HTTP route to client call, test to code, table reads and writes, and co-change history) loaded into SQLite tables with indexes in both directions.
LadybugDB (graph)
typed store (graph engine)
Reads
The same fact files as the relational store.
Answers
Native graph queries for the walk; the same ranking and packer. Its best native form (its best form) adds a stored Louvain community (a standard clustering) grouping to personalised PageRank; the PageRank iterations themselves run in application code.
What it is
The same facts loaded into LadybugDB, an embedded property-graph database with typed variable-length paths and a graph-algorithm extension (PageRank, Louvain communities, k-core).
DuckDB graph layer
typed store (graph engine)
Reads
The same fact files as the relational store.
Answers
Property-graph and recursive SQL queries for the walk; the same ranking and packer. Its k-hop walk is recursive SQL, not a graph operator.
What it is
The same facts in DuckDB with the DuckPGQ extension, which adds property-graph queries (bounded variable-length paths, PageRank) to SQL.
Reference implementation (check only)
cross-check only · not scored
Reads
The same fact files.
Answers
Computes the same walk and ranking directly. Used only to check that the three stores return the same answers; never scored.
What it is
A plain JavaScript implementation of the shared specification, holding the graph in memory.
Scripted search
baseline
Reads
The source tree exported at the parent commit, plus (for its padding tail) the earlier commit history.
Answers
Ranks files by weighted hit counts, then appends a co-change padding tail (files that changed together with the seed file in earlier commits); same packer and budget as every arm.
What it is
A fixed script modelled on the searches sessions run: text search of the source tree for the seed's names, its module path and its route strings, then follows the seed's import lines. It is a fixed script, not an agent searching with judgement.
Changed-together floor
floor
Reads
Earlier commit history (ancestor commits only, same-PR siblings dropped) and file paths.
Answers
Prints those files in that order with their signature lines until the budget is used. Every method must beat it.
What it is
No search and no graph: the files that changed together with the seed file in earlier commits, most often and most recently first, then the same-directory order.
Same-folder floor
floor
Reads
File paths at the parent commit.
Answers
Prints those files in that order with their signature lines until the budget is used. Every method must beat it.
What it is
No search and no graph: the files in the seed's own directory, then its capability folder, then outward.
One task, as a picture
1. Seed
One file and one symbol that a real commit changed. The tool sees the code as it was just before that commit.
→
2. The tool's answer
A ranked list of files, each printed as its path and the signature lines of its definitions, packed until
8,000 tokens
are used. Every tool gets the same packer and the same budget.
→
3. Compared with the commit
The other files and definitions the same commit changed (the reference) are counted:
recall
= found ÷ all of them.
Recall
is the share of the files and definitions the real change touched that the tool put in front of the reader. As scored, it is the share of reference files and definitions (those the same commit changed) that appear inside an 8,000-token answer, averaged over a commit's tasks and then over commits. A definition counts only when its own lines are shown. Every item counts the same: recall does not weigh how important an item was, and it does not count context a change needed but did not edit.
Three worked examples (tuning tasks, never held-out)
Example
typed store clearly beats search
tie
search or a floor beats the typed store
1. The seed
2. What the commit changed
3. Typed store answer
4. Scripted search answer
5. Changed-together floor answer
6. The score
Step 1. The seed: the one changed file the tool is given
Seed file
technician-app/src/features/jobs/CompleteJobScreen.tsx
, starting at the component
CompleteJobScreen
.
The real commit changed 8 files in total. The tools see only the code as it was just before the commit, and must find the rest.
← Back
Next →
From the 388 tuning change-completion tasks whose reference has 3 to 8 units (files plus definitions), so that an example fits on the page: (1) typed clearly beats search = typed recall minus search recall at 8,000 tokens of at least 0.30; (2) tie = typed, search and the co-change floor all have the same recall, strictly between 0 and 1; (3) search or the co-change floor beats typed = the better of the two minus typed of at least 0.30. In each group the tasks are sorted by that difference (for the tie, by reference size), then by task id, and the median task is taken (the lower one when the count is even). Never held-out.
The task sets and their sizes
Task set
Tuning
Held-out
Change completion (primary set)
(T1)
Given one changed file and symbol of a real commit as the seed, find the other files and definitions that the same commit changed.
388 tasks, 65 commits
418 tasks, 75 commits
Cross-runtime subset of change completion
(T1x)
The change completion tasks from commits that changed more than one runtime (mobile app, backend, web); descriptive only because there are few commits.
144 tasks, 17 commits
82 tasks, 9 commits
Tests to update
(T2)
Given changed production code as the seed, find the existing test files the same commit changed.
240 tasks, 47 commits
237 tasks, 49 commits
Documented chains
(T3)
Chains of connected files written down in the project's own documents (the lifecycle audit, the open-work handoff and the invariants in CLAUDE.md): seed = the first named file, reference = the others, with the document removed from the facts.
none
25 tasks, 4 parent commits, 3 documents
Defect register to fix files
(T4)
Defects recorded in the project's handoff register: seed = the named file, reference = the files of the commit that fixed it. Descriptive only.
none
8 tasks, 4 fix commits
Code-reading questions
(TQ)
Structured questions whose answers are fixed by reading the code (for example which tables a screen writes, which tests reach a function, which invariants govern a file, which code reads a setting), in 8 kinds; each answer written by one reader, checked blind by a second, disagreements resolved by a third.
20 questions
30 questions
Document lookups (not run)
(T5)
Free-text lookups typed in past sessions. Not run (addendum 4 item 7): the seed contract maps a file or definition, and T5 had no tuning part on which a text-to-seed step could be built.
none
27 listed, not run
The frozen configuration of each tool (chosen on tuning, then fixed)
Tool
Role
What it does
Tuning recall
relational, LadybugDB and the DuckDB graph layer (the same configuration in all three)
primary
Walk from the seed over every base edge class (structure, imports, calls, HTTP, tests, data, settings and co-change history) in both directions with no hop limit, using edges of confidence 0.5 or more; rank reached files by degree-normalised spreading (each node passes its score on, divided by its number of edges); show files with their signature lines; no padding tail.
0.785
relational, LadybugDB and the DuckDB graph layer
the 4-hop choice (the choice of the tie rule as first written)
The same walk limited to 4 hops, ranked by personalised PageRank (restart 0.30, co-change weight 1). The rule as first written picked it because "hops=4" sorted first by name among 15 tied configurations.
0.768
LadybugDB
best form (best form regardless of complexity)
Unbounded walk; personalised PageRank fused with the stored Louvain community (a standard clustering) grouping of the engine as a locality prior. Scored on the stored grouping and on four further community draws.
0.786
scripted search
primary
One round of text search for the seed's names and module path (route strings off), seed imports followed, then the co-change padding tail.
0.759
scripted search
best form
One round with route strings on, co-change ranking fused into the search ranking by reciprocal rank fusion, then the co-change padding tail.
0.764
same-directory floor
primary
File granularity, signature form, same-directory order.
0.719
co-change floor
primary
File granularity, signature form, co-change order then same-directory order.
0.752
primary
: The configuration each arm froze by the selection rule: highest change completion tuning primary, then the simplest configuration within one standard error, ties broken by higher tuning primary (addendum 4). All verdicts rest on these rows.
the 4-hop choice
: The choice the tie rule would have made as first written (ties broken by median tokens, then by name, which picked "hops=4"). Run on held-out only so the change of rule can be checked; no claim rests on it.
best form
: The arm's highest tuning primary regardless of complexity, where it differs from the primary: LadybugDB (native community prior) and search. Secondary comparisons the community-prior result and the typed-store verdict against search best form.
What it shows.
Every tool answered the same tasks under the same rules: one seed, one shared packer, an 8,000-token answer, and a score that counts what the real commit changed. In the three examples: typed store clearly beats search — typed store 4 of 5, scripted search 2 of 5, changed-together floor 2 of 5; tie — typed store 2 of 5, scripted search 2 of 5, changed-together floor 2 of 5; search or a floor beats the typed store — typed store 2 of 4, scripted search 4 of 4, changed-together floor 4 of 4.
C
Which tool put more of the real change in front of the reader?
How to read it.
First chart: each tool's recall at 8,000 tokens with its interval; the three engines' rows are the same frozen configuration. Second chart: each difference with its 95 % interval, paired commit by commit; a bar clear of zero and of the grey band is a difference worth acting on. The task-set switch changes both charts; the answer-size switch changes the second one. Third: recall at every answer size, the token-efficiency view. Rows are faded where the set is too small for an effect size.

What it shows.
On change completion the typed store's answer held 0.700 of the change, the changed-together floor 0.638, scripted search 0.625 and the same-folder floor 0.549. Against scripted search the typed store is ahead at every answer size scored: +0.070 [+0.029, +0.113] at 2,000 tokens, +0.102 [+0.068, +0.137] at 4,000 and +0.068 [+0.035, +0.105] at 16,000. Every answer fills its budget (median 7999 tokens), so this is "more found per token", not "fewer tokens spent".

What it shows.
The typed store's lead over scripted search is +0.151 [+0.097, +0.207] on mobile-app seeds and +0.012 [−0.038, +0.065] on backend seeds. Mobile seeds rose from 17 % to 36 % of tasks, so part of the overall gap comes from the mix. Against the changed-together floor the lead is the most fragile: dropping the five commits most favourable to the typed store leaves +0.029 [−0.008, +0.068], and seeds matched by id only give +0.060 [−0.001, +0.118]. About half of the typed store's own recall travelled over a changed-together edge.
E
Does the kind of database change the answer?
How to read it.
Top: one square per held-out task, for each engine; filled means the answer shown to the reader was identical to a plain in-memory reference implementation. Middle: the graph-only ranking (a stored community grouping added to personalised PageRank) against SQLite, for the stored grouping and four further draws, on five views. Bottom: every engine-native mode tried during tuning, as a change from the same walk written once for all engines.
SQLite (relational)
shown answers identical: 770 of 770 tasks
whole ranked list identical: 765 of 770 tasks
LadybugDB (graph)
shown answers identical: 770 of 770 tasks
whole ranked list identical: 770 of 770 tasks
DuckDB graph layer
shown answers identical: 770 of 770 tasks
whole ranked list identical: 767 of 770 tasks
Code-reading questions: LadybugDB 30 of 30 and DuckDB 30 of 30 identical to SQLite.
The first-written 4-hop configuration: 770 of 770 tasks for all three and the reference arm.
Tuning: SQLite (relational) against LadybugDB: 44 shared configurations over stages 1 and 2, all 33,968 tuning answers identical.
Tuning: SQLite (relational) against the DuckDB graph layer: 44 shared configurations, 33,962 of 33,968 identical; the other 6 answers differ only by one stored score at the twelfth decimal (0.01059323928 against 0.010593239279); items and order identical.
Tuning: The configuration first chosen (the 4-hop choice, 4 hops): 772 tuning answers identical across relational, reference, LadybugDB and the DuckDB graph layer (2,316 of 2,316 comparisons).
Tuning: After the pre-flight code changes, all 80 stored tuning question answers and 390 sampled tuning task answers were reproduced exactly (addendum 5 item 4).
Why 5 SQLite and 3 DuckDB ranked lists differ beyond what is shown
The 8 (arm, task) pairs are 2 distinct queries: one seed at one parent commit appears in change completion, the cross-runtime subset and tests to update, and another in change completion and tests to update. In every pair the shown items (80 or 86 items, scores included), the budgets, the seeds and the 50-path sample beyond the budget are identical, and every score field is identical. The hash covers about 3,900 ranked nodes with their raw scores; the reference arm rounds scores to 12 decimals, so a last-digit difference in a deep-tail score or the order of two nodes with equal rounded scores, somewhere past position 130, changes it. The stored answers hold only the hash of that tail, so which of the two it was cannot be told from them. No reported measure reads beyond the items packed into the 16,000-token answer: the scorer reads only items, budgets and seeds.
The one graph-only ranking that reached the final
held-out, LadybugDB + community prior minus SQLite
Adding a stored community prior to personalised PageRank gave no gain of 0.05 recall or more on change completion. Stored draw +0.015 [-0.010, +0.042]; the five community draws give +0.009 to +0.032, and draw 1 alone (+0.032 [+0.004, +0.061]) would be "inconclusive" under the same rule. On tests to update the stored draw is -0.037 [-0.083, +0.007], not inside plus or minus 0.05. The PageRank iterations run in application code; only the community grouping comes from the engine. One configuration, one repository.

Was each engine given its best form?
tuning, 65 commits
Each of SQLite, LadybugDB, DuckDB and the scripted search had 60 configurations on the same 65 tuning commits (388 tasks); each floor had 8.
All arms ran in one Linux image, because LadybugDB's algorithm extension would not load on Windows and running without it would have handicapped the graph side.
The relational store had a strong design: integer ids, typed node and edge tables, indexes in both directions, depth-capped recursive queries and stored rank columns.
Audits before scoring fixed defects on both sides: a division by zero in a ranking, two engines defining weighted shortest path differently, communities that changed with each build, and DuckDB counting paths beyond the hop limit.
Best engine-native row per graph arm: LadybugDB personalised PageRank fused with its stored Louvain community (a standard clustering) prior (unbounded) 0.7862, 0.0009 above the frozen shared-specification primary 0.7853; the DuckDB graph layer PageRank-lifted personalised PageRank (unbounded) 0.7829. In the LadybugDB row the PageRank iterations run in application code; only the community grouping comes from the engine.
The DuckDB k-hop walk is recursive SQL, not a graph operator.
change in tuning recall against the same walk written to the shared specification (no interval: each row is one tuning score; standard errors in the hover)

What it shows.
No. The three implementations compute the same answers from the same facts with the same method. The only graph-only ranking that reached the final changed answers, and gave no gain of 0.05 recall or more: +0.015 [−0.010, +0.042] on change completion, draws from +0.009 to +0.032, and −0.037 on tests to update. This is one configuration on one repository, so it does not rule out a gain from other graph algorithms.
F
What changed the score: hops, facts or the ranking algorithm?
How to read it.
tuning set: optimistic
These numbers come from the 65 tuning commits the settings were chosen on, so they are higher than held-out. Press a hop setting: the long bar shows the tuning recall at that depth. The ranking chart uses the same scale, so the size of the two effects can be compared by eye. The last chart shows every setting that was swept.

change completion tuning primary = recall of reference files and definitions inside an 8,000-token answer, mean over the 65 tuning commits (388 tasks); se = standard error over commits. Paths-only forms have no primary (scored on file ranking only).
Starting point, typed store: 2 hops, both directions, the 8 base edge classes (structure, imports, calls, http, tests, data, config, history), personalised PageRank with restart 0.30 and co-change weight 1, no hub cut, file granularity, signature form, no padding, minimum edge confidence 0.5 (0.664). Frozen: unbounded hops, both directions, base edge classes, degree-normalised ranking, file granularity, signature form, no padding (0.785). Starting point, scripted search: two rounds of text search for the seed names, module path and route strings; import lines of the seed followed; no BM25; no padding; file granularity; signature form (0.502). Best scripted search without a padding tail:
bm25 top=100 rounds=3
(0.655).
What it shows.
How far the walk goes moved tuning recall by 0.346 (0.438 at 1 hop, 0.784 unbounded) and following edges in one direction only cost 0.215; which facts are stored mattered next (the history of files that changed together added 0.056). Every ranking mode, including PageRank, landed within 0.014 of each other at 2 hops and 0.023 at unbounded depth. For the scripted search, its padding tail moved recall most (0.237).
G
How did typed queries do on code-reading questions?
How to read it.
30 held-out questions whose answers are fixed by reading the code (for example which tables a screen writes, which tests reach a function, which invariants govern a file, which code reads a setting).
F1
scores one answer from 0 to 1: it is high only when most of the right items were given and most of the given items were right. Top: the difference in F1 with its 95 % interval. Middle: one row per question, the circle is the typed query and the square the scripted search. Bottom: tokens counted two ways.
Judgement items
with the 7 judgement items
without them
F1 difference, typed queries minus scripted search
held-out, 30, 23, 22 and 8 questions
Judgement items are the 7 answer items on held-out questions that both readers left out of the reference; "without" removes them from the answers.
grey band: ±0.05
F1 typed queries minus scripted search (held-out); bars: 95 % interval over questions
all 30 held-out questions
+0.100 [−0.060, +0.257]
23 questions (the untouched list as first written)
+0.189 [+0.049, +0.346]
22 questions untouched in every arm
+0.158 [+0.025, +0.309]
8 questions whose handling changed (descriptive)
−0.058 [−0.500, +0.367]
Verdict by the pre-declared rule:
inconclusive
. The rule decides on all 30 questions and turns inconclusive when the 23 or the 22 questions give a different verdict. On 23 and on 22 questions the interval excludes zero, but the rule's token condition holds only when the typed final answer is compared with what the scripted search read; on final answers alone it fails.
The rule and the verdict words, exactly as recorded
the verdict uses all 30 questions; if the 23 questions of the addendum 5 list or the 22 untouched in every arm give a different verdict or sign, the questions verdict is inconclusive and both are shown (addendum 5 item 5; addendum 6 item 3).
On 23 questions: better (typed) only when the typed final answer (median 40 tokens) is compared with what the scripted search read (median 819); on final answers alone (typed 40 against search 35 tokens) the token condition fails and the word is inconclusive.
On 22 questions:better (typed) only when the typed final answer (median 41.5 tokens) is compared with what the scripted search read (median 1008); on final answers alone (typed 41.5 against search 29.5 tokens) the token condition fails and the word is inconclusive.
Tokens, counted two ways
held-out, median per question
The size of the final answer, and what the scripted search read to reach it. The typed side's own costs are not counted: knowing the schema, and the query programs written once per kind of question on the 20 tuning questions.

Scoring convention: a non-empty answer to an empty reference counts as recall 1 (precision 0, F1 0); an empty answer to an empty reference counts 1 for all three ; F1 is the same under either recall convention.
8 questions had their handling changed after their text was read (in the typed arms' or the search's parser, a cut-back rule, or the LadybugDB setting fix). No reference answer was used for those changes.
Which questions changed, and how they scored
Question handling changed after the held-out question text was read for 8 questions: 24, 27, 33, 40, 41, 46 and 47 in the typed arms (parser), 27, 33, 41, 47 and 25 in search (parser), 40 (cut-back rule) and 47 (LadybugDB setting fix). Addendum 5 listed 7; question 25 was found later.
On the 7 first-listed touched questions the typed arms win 1, lose 2 and tie 4 (F1 against search). They score F1 0 on 4 of them (24, 40, 46, 47); on 24 and 40 search also scores 0. With question 25 added (8 touched questions) they win 2, lose 2 and tie 4.
All 30, with judgement items: typed macro F1 0.745, scripted search 0.645; median lookups 1 against 2. Tuning (20 questions): typed 0.811, search 0.742.
What it shows.
Typed queries scored +0.100 F1 higher on all 30 questions, with an interval [−0.060, +0.257] that includes zero, so the pre-declared verdict is inconclusive; on the 22 untouched questions the interval [+0.025, +0.309] excludes zero. They were weakest on "the other half of a rule" (typed 0.000 against 0.306). A typed query returns the answer directly; the scripted search read a median 694.5 tokens of search and file output to produce an answer of about the same size. Only the 8 known kinds of question were tested.
H
Which engine is faster, smaller and easier to work with?
How to read it.
timing run, tuning inputs only, 5 passes
A separate timing run, after the held-out run was scored, and independently audited. A dot is the median over 5 passes, the bar the spread from the fastest to the slowest pass, and the small ticks each pass. The time axis is logarithmic: each grid line is ten times the one before. The grey label beside each dot says where that ranking runs: in the engine, in SQL statements, or in application code. Switch the graph to see the larger frozen graph.
The plain reading
On the selected method, SQLite was fastest:
14.5 ms per ranking on the tuning graph and 32.7 ms on the frozen graph, against 41.5 and 71.2 ms for LadybugDB (graph) and 70.3 and 98.7 ms for the DuckDB graph layer (one thread, warm). These differences hold in wall and CPU time in all 5 passes.
On the PageRank variants, the arm that runs PageRank in application code was fastest:
frozen graph, 4-hop variant, 17.1 ms for LadybugDB (PageRank in JavaScript over a cached adjacency), 58.4 ms for the DuckDB graph layer and 256 ms for SQLite (about 270 SQL statements per ranking). This compares PageRank implementations, not graph operators.
Every time here is milliseconds; one model step takes seconds.
A model step takes about 6.6 s at the median, so engine speed does not enter the token bill. Answers were the same in every engine (section E).

How it was run, in the audit's words, with this page's names for the methods and engines
The engine timing ran after the held-out run was scored, on tuning inputs only: 200 tuning seeds on one June commit graph (2,450 nodes, 7,077 edges), 200 file nodes of the frozen commit graph (5,464 nodes, 16,462 edges) and the 20 tuning questions. No held-out task or question was timed.
The three engines took turns in short blocks inside one container on six pinned CPUs, one engine at a time (no two blocks overlapped in time), over five passes from 08:05 to 11:21 UTC on 2026-11-13; every engine was first in about a third of the rounds.
The PC was busy throughout: other work used a median of about 6 of its 24 logical CPUs during the blocks. 148 of the 2,148 block attempts in the passes were repeated because other work exceeded 12 CPUs, and 6 blocks were kept and flagged after three repeats; dropping them changes nothing.
All three engines returned the same ranked answer for every seed, in every pass, at every thread setting, warm and cold, and the same answers to the 20 questions.
An independent recomputation from the raw block files reproduced every number in the engine table and all 180 engine-to-engine verdicts.
For the selected configuration (the frozen primary), SQLite answered fastest: median 14.5 ms per ranking on the tuning graph and 32.7 ms on the frozen graph, against 41.5 and 71.2 ms for LadybugDB and 70.3 and 98.7 ms for the DuckDB graph layer (one thread, warm). These differences hold in wall and CPU time in all five passes.
Between LadybugDB and the DuckDB graph layer no difference is stated for the frozen primary warm median (71.2 against 98.7 ms): LadybugDB was lower in all five passes, but the 27.5 ms gap is smaller than the 36 ms spread between DuckDB's passes, so under the declared rule it stays inside the noise.
For the PageRank configurations (the 4-hop and the 2-hop variants), the arm that runs PageRank in JavaScript over a cached adjacency (LadybugDB) was fastest, and the arm that runs it as about 270 SQL statements per ranking (SQLite) was slowest: frozen graph, 4-hop variant: 17 ms, 58 ms and 256 ms (LadybugDB, DuckDB, SQLite). With the application caches dropped, LadybugDB is 51 ms and still lowest. This compares PageRank implementations, not graph operators.
For the 20 question lookups SQLite took about 0.3 ms per question, LadybugDB about 1.9 ms and DuckDB about 9 ms (warm median, frozen graph).
Building a store took about 0.35 to 0.9 s for SQLite, 0.6 to 0.7 s for DuckDB and 3.1 to 3.7 s for LadybugDB (median over five builds), and a one-file update 0.18 to 0.27 s for SQLite and DuckDB (no difference between them beyond the noise) against 0.52 to 0.63 s for LadybugDB; the stores hold different extra tables, so this compares the three designs, not the engines alone.
Every per-query time is a few milliseconds to a few hundred milliseconds on graphs of a few thousand nodes; the test does not show whether these differences matter for an agent working in a session.
Machine and versions:
Linux container, Node v24.22.3, 6 CPUs; SQLite 3.54.1 through node:sqlite; LadybugDB 0.22.3 through @ladybugdb/core; DuckDB through @duckdb/node-api 2.0.0-r.1, with the DuckPGQ community extension (extension build 9c11bc192a), all in the image graphtest:1. Source: the version string each timing worker recorded in the unit files of the timing run; the DuckPGQ extension build from the arm notes of the same image.
What these timings do not show: that a graph engine is faster or slower than SQL in general (the order depends on the method and on where its ranking runs); that LadybugDB's engine computes PageRank (it runs in JavaScript); that one engine should be adopted on this evidence; anything about held-out tasks, larger graphs, other repositories or other machines; or whether an agent finishes work faster.

survives
"Calibration pick" is the fewest threads within 3 % of the lowest median on 20 calibration seeds, picked once under load; it is not a setting shown to be faster in the passes. "Best" is the calibration pick (fewest threads within 3 % of the lowest median on 20 calibration seeds x 3, once, under load), not a setting shown to be faster in the passes. LadybugDB frozen primary "best 4" was 26 % faster in calibration, but in the passes it is 67.4 ms against 71.2 ms at 1 thread (spreads about 14 to 15 ms) and uses about 108 ms of CPU against 72 ms. SQLite's question "best 4" is PRAGMA threads = 3, sorter helpers only, picked on 0.261 against 0.234 ms.

Labels on where each method runs, as corrected by the audit (with this page's names)
needs qualification:
The DuckDB graph layer's k-hop is recursive SQL, not a graph operator (bounded hops: a recursive SQL statement; unbounded: iterativelength() over a CSR built in the statement).
Qualification:
The first clause ("recursive SQL, not a graph operator") is true for the bounded 4-hop and 2-hop methods only. For the frozen primary (unbounded) the DuckDB graph layer reaches nodes with iterativelength(), a graph function of the extension over a CSR, and fetches the reach DAG with a GRAPH_TABLE MATCH one-edge pattern (arms/duckpgq/pgq.js khop). The parenthesis says so; the headline clause should not be quoted alone.
survives:
LadybugDB's personalised PageRank runs in application code over a cached adjacency (40 iterations in JavaScript over a transition CSR fetched by one Cypher query and cached per configuration).
survives:
SQLite's walk is a recursive common table expression (bounded hops; unbounded hops use a level-wise loop of SQL statements driven by application code).
survives:
Where the frozen primary's ranking (degree-normalised spreading, addendum 2 item 20) runs: LadybugDB fetches arcs and out-degrees by Cypher and spreads the activation in application code (JavaScript), so for LadybugDB this ranking is not in the engine; SQLite and the DuckDB graph layer spread it in SQL, one statement per BFS level, the level loop driven by application code; SQLite's unbounded walk is likewise a level loop in application code.
What the audit could not do:
The reference-arm comparison of bench/icheck.js was not re-run (no arm or bench was executed by this audit); cross-engine identity was recomputed instead. The VM side of the per-block load (sampled inside the container, not saved to a file) could not be re-derived. The CPU time of LadybugDB's facts load is not recorded per cold row, so the facts-load variant was judged on wall time only.
What it shows.
With the same answers in every engine, the choice between them is about cost, and every cost here is small: milliseconds per ranking on graphs of a few thousand nodes, and well under a second to update one file. On the selected method SQLite (relational) was fastest; on the PageRank variants the arm that runs PageRank in application code was fastest, which compares implementations, not graph operators. Store sizes and build times compare three designs that keep different extra tables. A model step takes about 6.6 s, so none of this moves the token bill.
I
How far can this result be trusted?
How to read it.
The timeline runs top to bottom; an orange dot marks a step where an audit caught something, with what it caught and what changed. Then the two changes made after tuning data existed, the fall from tuning to held-out for every tool, and the first-written tie rule.
Every audit, in order
Protocol frozen
2026-11-12, before any arm was built
What is varied (facts, algorithm, output, engine), the task sets, the arms, the scoring and the statistics were written down. Held-out tasks (75 commits from July and August) were set aside to be run once.
Environment fixed (addendum 1)
2026-11-12
Every arm moved into one Linux image after LadybugDB's algorithm extension would not load on Windows; running the graph side without it would have handicapped it.
Caught:
A handicap to one arm on Windows.
Changed:
One Linux image for all arms; facts still built on Windows from the same files.
Facts built
2026-11-12
One shared extractor built typed nodes and edges at each task's parent commit, cached by commit; both stores load the same files.
Phase A audits (addendum 2)
2026-11-12
Audits of scoring, facts and the search arm before any configuration was chosen.
Caught:
The old scoring rule credited a bare list of every code file with 0.94 recall, so it could not tell arms apart; the search arm had selected its own configuration on its own labels; extractor gaps (mobile config reads, SQL in strings, URLs in loops and ternaries, Protocol-typed calls); a co-change rule beyond the protocol.
Changed:
Content scoring (a definition counts only when its own lines are shown); one scorer run only by the grader; padding made a shared option every arm may use; search labels sealed and its self-selection voided; extractor fixed and every arm re-run.
Phase B audits (addendum 3)
2026-11-12 evening, before any arm was scored
Audits of the arms against the shared specification.
Caught:
Two arms divided by zero and two dropped a term in degree-normalised ranking at nodes with no out-edges; weighted shortest path defined differently in the two graph engines; LadybugDB communities differed with each store build; the DuckDB graph layer computed path lengths beyond the hop limit.
Changed:
One definition for each; communities stored once per graph and reused; the DuckDB graph layer fixed; equal stage-2 budgets (20 configurations per arm) and a parsimony rule; held-out comparisons declared in advance.
Tuning (stages 1 and 2)
2026-11-12 evening
60 configurations for each of relational, LadybugDB, the DuckDB graph layer and search (40 one factor at a time, then 20 proposed from each arm's own table), 8 for each floor, all on the 65 tuning commits.
Selection audit (addendum 4)
2026-11-13, before any held-out run
Audit of how configurations were chosen.
Caught:
The tie measure (median tokens) was inert because every answer fills its budget, so the choice fell to alphabetical order and picked "hops=4", the weakest of 15 tied configurations.
Changed:
Ties broken by higher tuning primary instead; the first choice kept as a shown extra row and run on held-out so the change can be checked; harness records keyed by arm and configuration.
Three pre-flight audits (addendum 5)
2026-11-13 morning
Checks of the run plan, the lock and the question code before the held-out run.
Caught:
Rounds 1 and 2 refused the run: the question parsers threw on 7 held-out questions in the typed arms and 4 in search; LadybugDB returned "::" where SQL returns nothing for a setting read outside any definition; a question naming a member with no node answered empty.
Changed:
One shared question parser; the LadybugDB translation fixed to match SQL; a cut-back rule for missing members; every tuning answer reproduced and all records re-frozen. Round 3 passed. Question 40 is still answered empty by all three typed arms (addendum 6 item 6).
Held-out run
2026-11-13 05:55 to 06:40 UTC
89 of 89 declared invocations ran once each; no repeat; input hashes unchanged before and after; reference answers hidden from every arm process; the live working tree, with the full git history and untracked copies of the sealed notes and statistics but no held-out answer or id, was mounted read-only (addendum 6 item 7).
Scoring
2026-11-13 from 06:43 UTC
The grader marked scoring started after checking the inventory, scored every answer file once with the same scorer as tuning, and ran the declared comparisons.
Three refuters (addendum 6)
2026-11-13
An independent re-scorer, a run-discipline auditor and a claims refuter.
Caught:
Re-scorer: 1,151 values and 46,200 per-task values reproduced; no verdict changes. Discipline: 8 touched questions, not 7; the full ranked-list hash differs on 5 relational and 3 the DuckDB graph layer tasks beyond the shown items; containers mounted the live working tree read-only; two containers ran at once. Claims: the change completion advantage over search and the co-change floor comes from mobile-app seeds; search is half co-change padding; the community-prior result depends on the community draw; question tokens were counted differently; one notes sentence was wrong (corrected here).
Changed:
No answer, score or verdict changed. Reporting rules bound (addendum 6 section C); this data file applies them.

Changes made after tuning data existed
Selection tie rule
— after the tuning tables existed, before any held-out run (addendum 4). The old tie measure was inert and the choice fell to alphabetical order.
The first-written choice scored 0.011 higher on held-out [-0.004, +0.026]; shown, supports no claim about either rule.
Question code
— after the held-out question text was read, before any held-out question was run (addendum 5; corrected in addendum 6). Parsers threw on held-out question phrasing; a LadybugDB translation defect; a cut-back rule for members with no node.

Reproduced
An independent scorer reproduced 1,151 point values to four decimals and all 46,200 per-task values; no verdict word changes.
This v2 recomputation reproduced 21 of 21 stored comparison rows exactly (difference and interval, change completion, tests to update and documented chains).
An independent check of this data file by the grader corrected its wording; no answer, score or verdict changed.
Further limits the grader recorded
Median answer tokens are 7,999 for every arm and set, so the 'no higher tokens' condition of the wording rule was always a tie and never decided a verdict.
documented chains references carry no commit field, so score.js clusters documented chains by task (25 clusters) although they come from 4 parent commits and 3 documents; documented chains intervals are probably too narrow. 20 of the 25 documented chains removals were no-ops by design (the document did not yet exist at the parent).
the cross-runtime subset (9 commits) and the defect register (8 tasks, 4 fix commits) are descriptive only; their intervals are very wide.
the questions verdict applies the item-18 wording to macro F1 over questions (interval of the F1 difference, median tokens per question, sign over change completion, tests to update, documented chains and questions); the typed-store verdict's question signal uses the all-30 F1 difference with judgement items.
The share of reference definitions with no node uses my own matching rule (symbol node of the same path starting on the definition's first line, or same last name containing that line): held-out change completion 354 of 3,150 (11.2 %), variables and objects 252 (8.0 %); the same rule on tuning gives 212 variables and objects (5.1 %) where the audit reported 182 (4.4 %) by its method, so the two counts are not strictly comparable.
Runtime split is by the seed file's top folder (backend/, src/ = web, technician-app/ = mobile); web has only 7 commits.
The held-out judgement items (7) were taken from sealed/tq_agreement.txt; all 7 are items both readers left out, so removing them only removes answer items.
What it shows.
The run was declared in advance, ran once (89 of 89 invocations, no repeat), and was re-scored independently with no verdict changing. Every tool lost 0.054 to 0.170 recall from tuning to held-out, the typed-store rows less than scripted search and the floors. The weak points are named: 8 questions changed after their text was read, the change of tie rule (its first choice scored +0.011 [−0.004, +0.026] higher, which supports no claim about either rule), and the different mix of held-out changes.

What does this not show, and what could be tested next?
How to read it.
First the four things this test does not show, as the reporting rules list them. Then each possible next test as a decision you may take. None of them is planned. The wider test that would measure most of what you asked is drawn once, in K, and not repeated here.
The wider test, drawn as a design in K →
An agent baseline with structure, meaning, time and cached conclusions, and graphify as shipped, each as a switch; phases with a stop after each. It includes the agent pilot and the embedding arm below.
Not shown by this test
Whether an agent finishes a task faster or with fewer mistakes with any of these tools. No agent was run.
Context that a change needed but did not edit. The reference is what the commit changed.
Any other repository.
Engine speed, size and effort: now measured on tuning inputs only (see
H
); not on held-out tasks, larger graphs or other machines.
Run an agent pilot?
Would tell you
Whether an agent replaying real fix commits finishes faster, or with fewer mistakes, with the typed store than with scripted search. This is the question the harness decision rests on.
Needs
Model tokens and many agent runs; the owner decides the budget.
Why it matters
This test measured what a tool shows within a fixed answer size, not what an agent does with it.
Add an embedding-search arm?
Would tell you
Whether a vector search over the code, the strongest non-graph baseline in the literature, matches the typed store without a hand-built fact extractor.
Needs
A model download, which is the owner’s decision; nothing was installed for this test.
Why it matters
The typed store’s lead was over a fixed script and the changed-together floor, not over the strongest baseline.
Repeat on another repository?
Would tell you
Whether the lead that sits in mobile-app changes here is about this codebase or about typed stores in general.
Needs
Another repository with enough history and languages the extractor reads.
Why it matters
One repository; the whole gain sat in one part of it.
Re-run over several split dates?
Would tell you
A firmer estimate from the same repository, by scoring several tuning and held-out splits in turn (a secondary analysis the protocol allows).
Needs
Compute only; no new held-out tasks exist in this repository.
Why it matters
With 75 held-out commits the smallest detectable difference is about 0.09, so several comparisons stayed inconclusive.
What it shows.
Because the three engines gave identical answers, choosing between them is a question of cost, and the timing run (H) found every cost in milliseconds. Whether a typed store of connections helps an agent at all is a different question; only agent runs can answer it, and K draws the test that would.

your tokens
Not measured

Wider test
K0: replay the 190 snapshots under five cache policies, with no model and no tokens. K1 and A3c with an agent only if K0 passes; A3s is the same store with invalidation switched off. Your prompt cache already serves 94.9 % of FixFlow context tokens as cache reads. Those are still tokens the model processes: the prompt cache lowers price and delay, not the token count (protocol-v2.md). A database cache that keeps unneeded content out of the window would therefore cut cached and uncached tokens alike; how much is untested. The token report also measured that only 0.5 to 1.5 % of a window is content fetched a second time inside the same window, so a cache that only stops repeat reads within one session would save little; stopping a first, unneeded read is the untested part.

2 · Graphify, as a picture
What it builds
Read from its source code (version 0.9.108). "Same family" means a parser-built code graph like the one tested here.
=
Code layer
Files, classes, functions, methods, imports, calls, inheritance, SQL tables and foreign keys, config keys. The same family as the graph this test used. From: tree-sitter parsers, about 40 languages. Model tokens to build: none.
SAME FAMILY AS TESTED
+
Model-read layer
Concept nodes, rationale text, document-to-code links, "semantically similar" edges, each with a confidence label. From: a model reads every uncached document, PDF and image. Model tokens to build: yes.
NOT IN THIS TEST
+
Clusters and report
Hub nodes, surprising cross-cluster links, knowledge gaps, suggested questions. From: Leiden communities named by a model; a report file GRAPH_REPORT.md. Model tokens to build: names only.
NOT IN THIS TEST
+
Always-on pointer
Tells the agent to query the graph before searching or reading. From: a CLAUDE.md block and hooks before search and read calls. Model tokens to build: none.
NOT IN THIS TEST
No time validity, no history and no stored summaries; invalidation is per file by content hash. Queries are lexical seed-finding followed by breadth-first expansion, cut at 2,000 tokens by default. The tested graph has changed-together history, route-to-call and table read/write edges that graphify lacks (its changed-together analysis is an open pull request, #4544).

Real headless coding-agent runs; the graphify arm is exactly what its installer produces. Intervals resample tasks only, so run-to-run noise is unmeasured. Against a baseline with subagents disabled, code tasks on the strong model cost +107k tokens [+38k, +180k] with graphify. Contradiction hunt: 4 tasks, graphify found 4 of 4 against 2 of 4 (F1 0.94 against 0.68), on 12 contradictions the benchmark's author planted. On doc-and-code questions the strong model's cost fell by 0.084 US dollars [-0.180, -0.005].
Refuter downgrade:
The refuter downgrades the doc-and-code positive to weak and directional: 80 % against 70 % on 20 tasks is a difference of 2 tasks with no accuracy interval, the token interval crosses zero, the weaker model reverses the sign (75 % against 80 %), and the arms differ by more than the graph. The page itself states both 65 and 105 for the task count.

A knowledge layer acts on the fetched-content part and on repeated lookups. It is at most the third lever, after capping the long orchestrator window and stripping the fixed prefix.
Cap the long orchestrator window at 150k to 200k tokens
39 % (FixFlow) and 74 % (Clinic) of cache-read tokens sit in calls whose context had passed 200k
Strip the fixed prefix of subagents
about 60k tokens per agent window: 27 % of all tokens, 47 % of FixFlow's
Fewer lookup steps and less fetched content: where a knowledge layer acts
fetched content is 26 % of context tokens; calls that only read or search are 64 % of FixFlow's calls and re-read 55 % of its tokens
These are exposures: the share of tokens a lever touches, not what it would save. They overlap, and most are cache reads; whether cache reads count against your limits is unknown. Source: docs/program/TOKEN-BOTTLENECK-2026-11.md (every API call in this PC's transcripts for FixFlow and the Clinic Workshop repo, 35,834 calls).
DESIGN, NOT A RESULT · nothing here has been run
The wider test, in one picture
A design, not a result. Nothing in it has been built, run, installed or frozen; it is a draft for your decisions, with the corrections of the design check applied.
Question:
On this repository, does a knowledge layer let an agent do the same work with fewer total tokens and no loss of correctness, and which layer earns its cost?
Primary outcome:
Total tokens per task over all model calls, subagents included, plus the layer's build and update tokens spread over the tasks it served, plus its tool descriptions in every call; correctness against sealed references as a guard.

Controls beside them: A0p, a placebo tool with a description of equal length; A0x, a plain capped keyword index; A3s, the cached-conclusion store with invalidation switched off. Optional, your decision: E, an embedding entry point (a model download); T, Graphiti as shipped on set K only.
What it can conclude:
Graph type (graph, knowledge graph, temporal knowledge graph) gets descriptive step contrasts with intervals; only A1, A3 and G2 against A0 can carry a verdict, unless you make the step contrasts primary (design check P1). A "cannot tell" does not mean a layer does not help.
Phases, with a stop after each
Phase 0
Feasibility
no agent, no model tokens
Do tests and type checkers run at old parents; how many change commits survive; how much needed-but-not-edited context each commit has; enough fresh commits?
STOP
if fewer than 25 runnable commits, or the needed-but-not-edited column is near empty.
Correction:
Design check P9: main has 0 product-code commits after the freeze, so the held-out set must be prospective: the first usable commits after the protocol is hashed, plus 14 days.
Phase 1
Screens
no agent, no model tokens
Break oracle by script; K0 cache replay over the 190 snapshots under five policies (none, whole repository, file hash, fact level, fact level with early cutoff); replay of the 139 recorded lookups.
STOP
and fix if a fact-level policy serves any stale answer.
Correction:
Design check P5: the draft stopped on any stale answer, but the file-hash policy is expected to serve some; its stale rate is a finding. The engine timing the check asked for here has now run (section H).
Phase 2
Model layers
model tokens; your decision 2
Build the meaning layer (A2) and graphify's document layer (G2) twice on 10 parents.
STOP
if build cost exceeds the agreed budget, or the two builds disagree on more than a quarter of anchored claims.
Correction:
Design check P10: A3 needs the knowledge store built first, and your D1 audit and D1b yes come before any build.
Phase 3
Pilot agent runs
agent runs
12 change tasks with 4 arms and 3 runs each; 30 questions with 5 arms and 2 runs each. Then freeze sample size, thresholds and arms, and hash the protocol.
STOP
if adoption is below 20 % in every tool arm without steering, or the leak audit finds leaks.
Correction:
Design check P2 and P3: as drafted, only savings of 20 % or more are detected reliably, and the correctness guard passes a harmless arm only 51 to 64 % of the time; choose the margins before the pilot ends.
Phase 4
Held-out main run, once
agent runs
40 change tasks with 4 arms and 3 runs; 70 questions with 6 arms and 2 runs; 80 break-oracle changes by script, then 30 with an agent.
RULE
Verdicts only by the rules fixed in advance.
Correction:
Design check P4: the stale-answer bound needs about 150 cache hits, so it is computed on set K (600 claim-steps), not on change tasks.
Phase 5
Score and report
scoring, three refuters
Score by script where the answer is a set or a test result; a judge only for rubric questions; three refuters; the must-not-claim lists bind the report.
Correction:
Design check P13: results speak to product-code tasks only; no verdict is possible on design and document work, where most measured spend is.
Cost, if it ran:
the draft estimated 0.17 to 0.55 billion tokens for the pilot and 0.6 to 2.0 billion tokens for the main run; the design check puts them at about 0.5 to 1.8 billion tokens and about 2 to 6.5 billion tokens. In this harness an agent call averages 113k to 123k tokens of context, about 60k of it fixed prefix, so the draft's per-run figure is likely 2 to 4 times too low. All recorded FixFlow context tokens so far are 2.13 billion. Phases 0 and 1 cost no model tokens.
Sources: graph2/widen/design-draft.md (the draft) and graph2/widen/design-check.md (the check, whose corrections are applied above).
What it shows.
This test answered the storage-engine question and the structure question. Your other questions (meaning read from documents, time with cached conclusions, an agent's tokens) were not measured here. Graphify's headline compares one capped answer with reading everything; independent controlled runs on code with a strong model found no clear gain, and its one positive sits in the document layer this test left out. In your sessions fetched content is about a quarter of context tokens; a knowledge layer acts there and on repeated lookups, behind two larger levers. The wider test is a design for your decision, not a result.
Sources: the single held-out run on 75 commits from July and August 2026, scored once; the 65 tuning commits from June 2026 on which every configuration was chosen. Data file generated by sealed/grader/heldout/v2/build_data.js (grader lane); independent checks passed: every comparison difference and interval equals sealed/grader/heldout/comparisons.json; the v2 recomputation reproduces the stored comparison rows exactly; no question text and no held-out question reference item; every worked example is a tuning task and its recall recomputed from the shown items equals the stored score; no held-out task id except the root-level task named by the reporting rule and the 5 tasks of the ranked-hash note; no held-out reference definition; engines left as a placeholder.
Wording rule used for every verdict: Better: the 95 % interval of the change completion difference excludes zero, the winner's median answer tokens are no higher, and the sign agrees on at least three of change completion, tests to update, documented chains and the questions (floors: change completion, tests to update, documented chains). Equivalent: the whole interval lies inside plus or minus 0.05 recall. Otherwise inconclusive. Intervals: paired cluster bootstrap over commits, 10,000 resamples, seed 20261112.
Reporting rules: Addendum 6 section C binds every summary: the engine comparisons are worded as the three implementations computing the same answers; the community-prior result as no gain of 0.05 recall or more from adding a stored community prior; every the typed-store verdict verdict is shown with the runtime split and the tuning-to-held-out mix change; search is called a scripted search; question tokens are shown two ways; Documented chains carry a sign only; the refuters' must-not-claim sentences may not appear.

4-hop variant (first-written rule) minus the frozen choice, change completion at 8,000 tokens: +0.011 [−0.004, +0.026]

4-hop variant (first-written rule) minus the frozen choice, area under the recall curve: +0.001 [−0.010, +0.012]
Code tasks, strong model (Sonnet 5): token difference per task 32k, interval [-8k, 75k]; accuracy 78 % with graphify against 84 % without; 45 tasks, one repetition each
Code tasks, weaker model (Haiku 4.5): token difference per task -119k, interval [-245k, -12k]; accuracy 82 % with graphify against 80 % without; 45 tasks, one repetition each
Doc-and-code questions, strong model: token difference per task -129k, interval [-325k, 33k]; accuracy 80 % with graphify against 70 % without; 20 tasks, one repetition each
Doc-and-code questions, weaker model: token difference per task -161k, interval [-317k, -25k]; accuracy 75 % with graphify against 80 % without; 20 tasks, one repetition each
fixed prefix re-read every call (system prompt, tool schemas, CLAUDE.md, briefs): 27 % of all context tokens (FixFlow 47 %, Clinic 19 %)
the model's own earlier output carried forward: 26 % of all context tokens (FixFlow 15 %, Clinic 31 %)
content fetched by reads and searches: 26 % of all context tokens (FixFlow 23 %, Clinic 27 %)
compaction summaries re-read: 8 % of all context tokens (FixFlow 2 %, Clinic 11 %)
shell output, messages, other: 13 % of all context tokens (FixFlow 13 %, Clinic 12 %)
B
What was compared, and how was each answer scored?
How to read it.
The cards are the seven tools: what each reads and how it answers. The three typed stores in the dashed box hold the same facts and run the same method. Below them, one task as a picture. At the bottom, step through three real tasks with Back and Next.
Typed stores: the same facts (files, functions, routes, tables, settings, tests and how they connect, including which files changed together before) in three engines
SQLite (relational)
typed store
Reads
Only the facts built from the parent commit of each task (never the task commit or anything later).
Answers
A recursive SQL query walks from the seed file and symbol over the typed edges, scores every reached file, and the shared packer prints the best files first as a path line plus the signature lines of their definitions until the token budget is used.
What it is
The extracted facts (typed nodes such as files, functions, routes, tables and settings, and typed edges such as imports, calls, HTTP route to client call, test to code, table reads and writes, and co-change history) loaded into SQLite tables with indexes in both directions.
LadybugDB (graph)
typed store (graph engine)
Reads
The same fact files as the relational store.
Answers
Native graph queries for the walk; the same ranking and packer. Its best native form (its best form) adds a stored Louvain community (a standard clustering) grouping to personalised PageRank; the PageRank iterations themselves run in application code.
What it is
The same facts loaded into LadybugDB, an embedded property-graph database with typed variable-length paths and a graph-algorithm extension (PageRank, Louvain communities, k-core).
DuckDB graph layer
typed store (graph engine)
Reads
The same fact files as the relational store.
Answers
Property-graph and recursive SQL queries for the walk; the same ranking and packer. Its k-hop walk is recursive SQL, not a graph operator.
What it is
The same facts in DuckDB with the DuckPGQ extension, which adds property-graph queries (bounded variable-length paths, PageRank) to SQL.
Reference implementation (check only)
cross-check only · not scored
Reads
The same fact files.
Answers
Computes the same walk and ranking directly. Used only to check that the three stores return the same answers; never scored.
What it is
A plain JavaScript implementation of the shared specification, holding the graph in memory.
Scripted search
baseline
Reads
The source tree exported at the parent commit, plus (for its padding tail) the earlier commit history.
Answers
Ranks files by weighted hit counts, then appends a co-change padding tail (files that changed together with the seed file in earlier commits); same packer and budget as every arm.
What it is
A fixed script modelled on the searches sessions run: text search of the source tree for the seed's names, its module path and its route strings, then follows the seed's import lines. It is a fixed script, not an agent searching with judgement.
Changed-together floor
floor
Reads
Earlier commit history (ancestor commits only, same-PR siblings dropped) and file paths.
Answers
Prints those files in that order with their signature lines until the budget is used. Every method must beat it.
What it is
No search and no graph: the files that changed together with the seed file in earlier commits, most often and most recently first, then the same-directory order.
Same-folder floor
floor
Reads
File paths at the parent commit.
Answers
Prints those files in that order with their signature lines until the budget is used. Every method must beat it.
What it is
No search and no graph: the files in the seed's own directory, then its capability folder, then outward.
One task, as a picture
1. Seed
One file and one symbol that a real commit changed. The tool sees the code as it was just before that commit.
→
2. The tool's answer
A ranked list of files, each printed as its path and the signature lines of its definitions, packed until
8,000 tokens
are used. Every tool gets the same packer and the same budget.
→
3. Compared with the commit
The other files and definitions the same commit changed (the reference) are counted:
recall
= found ÷ all of them.
Recall
is the share of the files and definitions the real change touched that the tool put in front of the reader. As scored, it is the share of reference files and definitions (those the same commit changed) that appear inside an 8,000-token answer, averaged over a commit's tasks and then over commits. A definition counts only when its own lines are shown. Every item counts the same: recall does not weigh how important an item was, and it does not count context a change needed but did not edit.
Three worked examples (tuning tasks, never held-out)
Example
typed store clearly beats search
tie
search or a floor beats the typed store
1. The seed
2. What the commit changed
3. Typed store answer
4. Scripted search answer
5. Changed-together floor answer
6. The score
Step 1. The seed: the one changed file the tool is given
Seed file
technician-app/src/features/jobs/CompleteJobScreen.tsx
, starting at the component
CompleteJobScreen
.
The real commit changed 8 files in total. The tools see only the code as it was just before the commit, and must find the rest.
← Back
Next →
From the 388 tuning change-completion tasks whose reference has 3 to 8 units (files plus definitions), so that an example fits on the page: (1) typed clearly beats search = typed recall minus search recall at 8,000 tokens of at least 0.30; (2) tie = typed, search and the co-change floor all have the same recall, strictly between 0 and 1; (3) search or the co-change floor beats typed = the better of the two minus typed of at least 0.30. In each group the tasks are sorted by that difference (for the tie, by reference size), then by task id, and the median task is taken (the lower one when the count is even). Never held-out.
The task sets and their sizes
Task set
Tuning
Held-out
Change completion (primary set)
(T1)
Given one changed file and symbol of a real commit as the seed, find the other files and definitions that the same commit changed.
388 tasks, 65 commits
418 tasks, 75 commits
Cross-runtime subset of change completion
(T1x)
The change completion tasks from commits that changed more than one runtime (mobile app, backend, web); descriptive only because there are few commits.
144 tasks, 17 commits
82 tasks, 9 commits
Tests to update
(T2)
Given changed production code as the seed, find the existing test files the same commit changed.
240 tasks, 47 commits
237 tasks, 49 commits
Documented chains
(T3)
Chains of connected files written down in the project's own documents (the lifecycle audit, the open-work handoff and the invariants in CLAUDE.md): seed = the first named file, reference = the others, with the document removed from the facts.
none
25 tasks, 4 parent commits, 3 documents
Defect register to fix files
(T4)
Defects recorded in the project's handoff register: seed = the named file, reference = the files of the commit that fixed it. Descriptive only.
none
8 tasks, 4 fix commits
Code-reading questions
(TQ)
Structured questions whose answers are fixed by reading the code (for example which tables a screen writes, which tests reach a function, which invariants govern a file, which code reads a setting), in 8 kinds; each answer written by one reader, checked blind by a second, disagreements resolved by a third.
20 questions
30 questions
Document lookups (not run)
(T5)
Free-text lookups typed in past sessions. Not run (addendum 4 item 7): the seed contract maps a file or definition, and T5 had no tuning part on which a text-to-seed step could be built.
none
27 listed, not run
The frozen configuration of each tool (chosen on tuning, then fixed)
Tool
Role
What it does
Tuning recall
relational, LadybugDB and the DuckDB graph layer (the same configuration in all three)
primary
Walk from the seed over every base edge class (structure, imports, calls, HTTP, tests, data, settings and co-change history) in both directions with no hop limit, using edges of confidence 0.5 or more; rank reached files by degree-normalised spreading (each node passes its score on, divided by its number of edges); show files with their signature lines; no padding tail.
0.785
relational, LadybugDB and the DuckDB graph layer
the 4-hop choice (the choice of the tie rule as first written)
The same walk limited to 4 hops, ranked by personalised PageRank (restart 0.30, co-change weight 1). The rule as first written picked it because "hops=4" sorted first by name among 15 tied configurations.
0.768
LadybugDB
best form (best form regardless of complexity)
Unbounded walk; personalised PageRank fused with the stored Louvain community (a standard clustering) grouping of the engine as a locality prior. Scored on the stored grouping and on four further community draws.
0.786
scripted search
primary
One round of text search for the seed's names and module path (route strings off), seed imports followed, then the co-change padding tail.
0.759
scripted search
best form
One round with route strings on, co-change ranking fused into the search ranking by reciprocal rank fusion, then the co-change padding tail.
0.764
same-directory floor
primary
File granularity, signature form, same-directory order.
0.719
co-change floor
primary
File granularity, signature form, co-change order then same-directory order.
0.752
primary
: The configuration each arm froze by the selection rule: highest change completion tuning primary, then the simplest configuration within one standard error, ties broken by higher tuning primary (addendum 4). All verdicts rest on these rows.
the 4-hop choice
: The choice the tie rule would have made as first written (ties broken by median tokens, then by name, which picked "hops=4"). Run on held-out only so the change of rule can be checked; no claim rests on it.
best form
: The arm's highest tuning primary regardless of complexity, where it differs from the primary: LadybugDB (native community prior) and search. Secondary comparisons the community-prior result and the typed-store verdict against search best form.
What it shows.
Every tool answered the same tasks under the same rules: one seed, one shared packer, an 8,000-token answer, and a score that counts what the real commit changed. In the three examples: typed store clearly beats search — typed store 4 of 5, scripted search 2 of 5, changed-together floor 2 of 5; tie — typed store 2 of 5, scripted search 2 of 5, changed-together floor 2 of 5; search or a floor beats the typed store — typed store 2 of 4, scripted search 4 of 4, changed-together floor 4 of 4.

Walk from the seed over every base edge class (structure, imports, calls, HTTP, tests, data, settings and co-change history) in both directions with no hop limit, using edges of confidence 0.5 or more; rank reached files by degree-normalised spreading (each node passes its score on, divided by its number of edges); show files with their signature lines; no padding tail.
0.785
relational, LadybugDB and the DuckDB graph layer
the 4-hop choice (the choice of the tie rule as first written)
The same walk limited to 4 hops, ranked by personalised PageRank (restart 0.30, co-change weight 1). The rule as first written picked it because "hops=4" sorted first by name among 15 tied configurations.
0.768
LadybugDB
best form (best form regardless of complexity)
Unbounded walk; personalised PageRank fused with the stored Louvain community (a standard clustering) grouping of the engine as a locality prior. Scored on the stored grouping and on four further community draws.
0.786
scripted search
primary
One round of text search for the seed's names and module path (route strings off), seed imports followed, then the co-change padding tail.
0.759
scripted search
best form
One round with route strings on, co-change ranking fused into the search ranking by reciprocal rank fusion, then the co-change padding tail.
0.764
same-directory floor
primary
File granularity, signature form, same-directory order.
0.719
co-change floor
primary
File granularity, signature form, co-change order then same-directory order.
0.752
primary
: The configuration each arm froze by the selection rule: highest change completion tuning primary, then the simplest configuration within one standard error, ties broken by higher tuning primary (addendum 4). All verdicts rest on these rows.
the 4-hop choice
: The choice the tie rule would have made as first written (ties broken by median tokens, then by name, which picked "hops=4"). Run on held-out only so the change of rule can be checked; no claim rests on it.
best form
: The arm's highest tuning primary regardless of complexity, where it differs from the primary: LadybugDB (native community prior) and search. Secondary comparisons the community-prior result and the typed-store verdict against search best form.
What it shows.
Every tool answered the same tasks under the same rules: one seed, one shared packer, an 8,000-token answer, and a score that counts what the real commit changed. In the three examples: typed store clearly beats search — typed store 4 of 5, scripted search 2 of 5, changed-together floor 2 of 5; tie — typed store 2 of 5, scripted search 2 of 5, changed-together floor 2 of 5; search or a floor beats the typed store — typed store 2 of 4, scripted search 4 of 4, changed-together floor 4 of 4.
C
Which tool put more of the real change in front of the reader?
How to read it.
First chart: each tool's recall at 8,000 tokens with its interval; the three engines' rows are the same frozen configuration. Second chart: each difference with its 95 % interval, paired commit by commit; a bar clear of zero and of the grey band is a difference worth acting on. The task-set switch changes both charts; the answer-size switch changes the second one. Third: recall at every answer size, the token-efficiency view. Rows are faded where the set is too small for an effect size.

What it shows.
On change completion the typed store's answer held 0.700 of the change, the changed-together floor 0.638, scripted search 0.625 and the same-folder floor 0.549. Against scripted search the typed store is ahead at every answer size scored: +0.070 [+0.029, +0.113] at 2,000 tokens, +0.102 [+0.068, +0.137] at 4,000 and +0.068 [+0.035, +0.105] at 16,000. Every answer fills its budget (median 7999 tokens), so this is "more found per token", not "fewer tokens spent".

C
Which tool put more of the real change in front of the reader?
How to read it.
First chart: each tool's recall at 8,000 tokens with its interval; the three engines' rows are the same frozen configuration. Second chart: each difference with its 95 % interval, paired commit by commit; a bar clear of zero and of the grey band is a difference worth acting on. The task-set switch changes both charts; the answer-size switch changes the second one. Third: recall at every answer size, the token-efficiency view. Rows are faded where the set is too small for an effect size.

What it shows.
On change completion the typed store's answer held 0.700 of the change, the changed-together floor 0.638, scripted search 0.625 and the same-folder floor 0.549. Against scripted search the typed store is ahead at every answer size scored: +0.070 [+0.029, +0.113] at 2,000 tokens, +0.102 [+0.068, +0.137] at 4,000 and +0.068 [+0.035, +0.105] at 16,000. Every answer fills its budget (median 7999 tokens), so this is "more found per token", not "fewer tokens spent".
